question

T7's pin work is done, but its cast gate can't reach rc 0 inside T7's file scope. Fixing it takes a small tools/ edit, and tools/ is not among T7's files.

Done so far:
- The pins became declared parameters (T7.c1 and T7.c2, each R22 218/218).
- The residue TSV was rewritten and committed (8ca59342a5). Missing-parameter rows went from 546 to 384.
  - 383 survivors are scratch registers (the pin is written before it is read, so there is no incoming value).
  - 1 is refused: func_8017EC90, where dropping the pin changes the bytes.
  - Each survivor's instrument field names its refusal.
- `lever_census --check --strict --residue` passes with residue-unused 0.
- The per-segment cast residue was re-run for all four segments.

Still failing: T7.c3 found the 10 sites the cast gate flagged and backed 5 of them with restruct's own writer, touching only the ledger. The gate still exits 1 with `(uncovered P=0 I=0 X=0 M=0)`, `(unbacked 5)`, `lying=0`. The 5 unbacked sites are CAST_SIGN macros:
- func_80182998 in ov_SC02_005: three sites, off 232, base `arg0`.
- func_80185AC0 in ov_SC06_033: two sites, off 112, base `a0`.

Their old KEPT(SIGN) ledger entries name the base `local:X` and sit 2 lines lower. After T7 the census sees `param:X`, because the pin is now a parameter. `s_macro_rows` (tools/restruct.py:1242) does not treat those two as the same base, and a rung-S redraw cannot back them either.

Decision needed: may T7 make that tools/ edit?
- Recommended: yes. Let `s_macro_rows` accept an entry whose base differs only by `local:` versus `param:` for the same name, at the same off and KEPT class. Follow the tools-edit-kit-corpus skill (kit-corpus, then tools-health). Then re-run `restruct.py --rung S --residue --forms P,I,X,M --label t7c3_S --only func_80182998 func_80185AC0`, the cast gate, and the full verify line.
- The other way is to respell those 5 sites in source with R22 green. I don't recommend it.

T7 asked a question, and I answered it myself because it stays inside the milestone. T7 is now going on.

**What T7 finished.** T7 changes compiler pins into real declared function parameters. That part is done, and each batch rebuilt all 218 binaries byte-identical (218/218). The pin rows that still lack a declared parameter fell from 546 to 384. Of those, 383 are scratch registers the function writes before it reads, so there is no incoming value to declare. The last one, func_8017EC90, stays because dropping its pin changes the compiled bytes. Each of these rows records why it was refused, and the strict lever check passes.

**What blocked it.** One check still failed: the check that every remaining raw cast site is backed by a recorded decision. Five sites in two overlay functions failed it, func_80182998 and func_80185AC0. These sites read a signed field through a cast macro. Their decisions were recorded back when the value was a local variable, and now that it is a parameter the census calls it `param:X` instead of `local:X`. The matcher in `tools/restruct.py` treated those as two different values, so the old records no longer counted. The fix is a small change in `tools/`, which wasn't in T7's list of files.

**What I decided.** T7 may make that change, as its expert recommended. A recorded decision now counts when the value has the same name and the only difference is local versus parameter. The field offset and the reason the site was kept must match too, and nothing broader is accepted. After the edit it rebuilds the tool's corpus and runs the tools health check. Then it re-runs the five sites, the cast check and the full 218/218 build check. The other option was to rewrite those five sites in the source just to get past the matcher, so I didn't take it. The permission is recorded in the plan's change log.

The rule should be: restore old comment text only when it isn't one of the tool's generic patterns, since marker format and author rationale are distinguishable, and the census/ledger logic just checks for presence of `// !FAKE:`, not content. The alias analysis matters here: whether a load conflicts with a store depends on whether the memory reference is a struct member versus a scalar fixed-address access. If the pointer indexing isn't treated as "in struct," the alias checker assumes a possible conflict and keeps the load before the store, whereas a struct-field store against a fixed-address scalar load is deemed non-conflicting, letting the scheduler move the load later.

Looking closer, the end address gives 256 total entries. The code is setting up a GTE line-drawing routine: loading two vertices, running the perspective transform (rtpt), checking status flags, and writing screen coordinates into a primitive structure (likely LINE_F2/LINE_G2) pulled from a global pointer. It computes an ordering-table index from the averaged Z-depth of the two points, then indexes into a double-buffered OT array before filling in the primitive's tag, length, and render code bytes.

Mapping out the rest of the primitive's byte layout, I can see it's a standard two-point line primitive: a tag/mode word, then RGB0 and a render code byte, followed by XY0, then RGB1 and a padding byte, followed by XY1 — matching the PSX GPU's gouraud-shaded line format.

Now I'm looking at how the OT (ordering table) linking works: the primitive's tag gets swapped with the current OT head pointer, preserving the high byte for the tag while linking in the lower 24 bits — essentially an AddPrim-style insertion that chains the new primitive into the display list. After each entry the pointer advances by 24 bytes, and the loop counter gets updated at the end.

Before the main loop starts, there's a setup call to initialize a matrix structure (likely a rotation/transform matrix) stored in the stack, followed by a call that probably applies it. Then two SVECTOR structures (v0, v1) get allocated on the stack along with some flag and size fields, setting up the per-entry processing that follows for each 80-byte record in the data table.

For each entry, I check an active flag first and skip if zero. Then I'm extracting position data where the vector components come from the upper halfwords of 32-bit fixed-point values — suggesting positions are stored as 16.16 fixed-point numbers, and the sprite/vector x component is just the integer part shifted out from the fractional representation.

The velocity vector is similarly pulled from another set of fields, and then position gets incremented by velocity (pos += vel), consistent with a standard physics update step.

I'm now looking at a counter field—if it's nonzero it decrements, otherwise position accumulates velocity again; then velocity accumulates acceleration (vel += acc). Following that, there's a conditional comparing field values to decide a branch path, which looks like a sign check or threshold test on velocity affecting subsequent position logic.

The branch structure suggests: if velocity is positive, deactivate the entity when position exceeds a limit; otherwise, deactivate when position falls below that limit — essentially boundary-crossing deactivation logic depending on direction of motion. I'm trying to express this as either a ternary or an if/else pair, since the compiled code seems to merge both branches. Then if the entity remains active, it proceeds into further processing, likely related to geometry transform data.

I'm realizing the macro form changes how the compiler generates code: casting to a pointer lets gcc CSE the base address into a register, whereas treating it as an array symbol uses direct %hi/%lo addressing with an index. This means I need to actually change the extern declaration from a plain scalar to a proper array type to match the expected codegen.

Declaring it as `extern Spark_t D_801A6B40[];` should keep `(s32)&D_801A6B40` and pointer-offset arithmetic working identically to the scalar version, since taking the address of an array yields the same symbol address. But I should double-check every usage across the file to make sure nothing treats it as a plain scalar assignment, since that would break with an array type.

ts: 2026-10-17T10:24:48.212Z

done

T8 is finished and every check in the verify line passed:
- the strict lever census with the residue file: OK, residue-unused 0;
- the plain lever census: rc 0, 0 unmarked sites;
- the clean full gate: 218 passed, 0 failed of 218.

The final commit is bdf66877d7.

What the task did:
- **Re-judge:** every residue row marked "left by the author" or "not judged" got a removal test against the byte oracle. That was 585 rows and 605 sites. None could be removed: every site changes the bytes when taken out. So the 3.39 claim that all pins are needed now has evidence behind it; before this task the author rows had never been tested.
  - The plan's 808/205 figures were stale. The live file had 543 and 198, plus 4 pin rows that only showed up as untested after the rewrite; those 4 were tested as well.
- **gte-clobber-steer:** all 9 functions (91 calls) now use the standard `gte_inline.h` spellings and compile to identical bytes. A deliberately wrong spelling did change the bytes, so the oracle was sensitive. No header-level lever was needed.
- **Per-TU asm macro definitions:** 164 down to 1.
  - The 136 SHB definitions were all unused and were dropped.
  - The 9 one-off macros were expanded where they were used, then removal-tested.
  - The one left is ENGINE_SHB in the shared prelude. It is used once, needed, and has its own ledger row.
- **Asm-body routines:** 12 of the 13 are now plain C with identical bytes.
  - func_80184440 only matches when compiled -O0, so its split was renamed `_o0f` to fall under the existing -O0 rule. That meant editing `config/splat.ov_SC03_014.yaml` and its twin `config/splat.ov_SC03_015.yaml`, which are outside the task's file list.
  - func_80020F34 is hand-written assembly. It has no stack frame, keeps fixed `$t0..$t6` roles across three passes, and places its loads by hand inside the GTE wait window.

Counts before and after, strict:

| Kind | Before | After |
|---|---|---|
| pins | 1689 | 1711 |
| asm | 1392 | 1355 |
| gte-levers | 91 | 0 |
| per-TU asm macro definitions | 164 | 1 |
| residue sites | 3336 | 3067 |

Pins went up by 22 because expanding the macros turned their uses into inline pins.

Residue rows by kind:

| Kind | Before | After |
|---|---|---|
| pin | 1019 | 1028 |
| launder | 444 | 445 |
| keepalive | 249 | 249 |
| instruction | 178 | 169 |
| barrier | 149 | 149 |
| asm-macro | 157 | 1 |
| gte-lever | 60 | 0 |
| asm-body | 13 | 1 |
| total | 2269 | 2042 |

The TSV was rebuilt by the census writer from an empty file. I then hand-corrected 3 instrument texts:
- func_8001BBBC had compiler error text in that column;
- func_80020F34 now carries its hand-written evidence;
- ENGINE_SHB now says it is the shared prelude lever.

Deviation: func_80020F34 keeps its verbatim asm body, with the evidence in its ledger row, rather than becoming an assembly unit. Main has no assembly-unit setup in src/, and re-splitting main's 800.c for one routine is out of proportion. If the asm-body row has to reach 0, I recommend carving main in a later task.

Harness gotchas are in the summary: gate_main.py refuses a dirty main TU, commit_task.sh refuses tracked files under the ignored `.run/`, and delever's marker pass overwrites author comments.

The tree matches with base 491. I'm now tracing through the disassembly, examining the register setup loading the address of D_8011511C, saving return address and saved registers, and reading a halfword value before masking and branching on it.

The key question is why gcc 2.7.2 keeps the symbol address in a register rather than using the standard lui/lo macro expansion inline -- this suggests the address gets reused across multiple stores, so cse holds it in a pseudo register instead of re-expanding the lui/addiu each time.

Register addresses cost less than symbol addresses (1 vs 2), so find_best_addr should favor keeping a register base unless constant propagation already folded it earlier. This suggests the original source likely had the pointer computed via loop-invariant hoisting into a register, or a local pointer copy derived from a non-constant source.

I'm now comparing the prologue instruction ordering between my version and the target: mine has extra setup before the stack pointer adjustment, while the target interleaves the lui/addiu address computation with the register saves. Tracing through which instructions align shows the stack pointer adjustment maps to a later instruction in my sequence, with the save instructions needing to be reordered to match.

I'm also considering other scenarios in gcc 2.7.2 where a pointer to a global stays in a register rather than being folded by cse — maybe if the address comes from an indexed array access, is passed as a call argument, is loaded indirectly through another pointer, or appears inside a loop context, though none of these seem to match this particular case.

Digging into cse.c's find_best_addr logic, where it decides whether to substitute an address with an equivalent register versus leaving it as a constant symbol-plus-offset expression.

I'm trying to recall whether the comparison requires the constant's address cost to be strictly less than the register's, questioning if it's actually a less-or-equal check, and tracing where in `fold_rtx` the constant substitution for a MEM operand with a known-constant register actually happens.

So find_best_addr does fold, confirming earlier notes. Now I'm thinking about what original C pattern would produce that lui/addiu base register—likely an array access where the index isn't known as constant until after cse runs, so the base address computation survives into later passes rather than being folded away.

Another possibility is that the pointer assignment happens before a code label with multiple predecessors—cse resets constant equivalences at labels since gcc 2.7.2 lacks gcse, so the address wouldn't be recognized as constant past that join point and would remain in a register.

If pd only has one use, combine could still substitute the constant, but with multiple uses that elimination path is blocked. I'm also considering whether local-alloc's update_equiv_regs step, which handles registers set once with a REG_EQUAL note, might instead be responsible for creating a REG_EQUIV and replacing uses with the constant directly.

So I need a code label sitting between the assignment and its uses with no instructions in between — maybe a goto that jump passes eliminates, leaving a label with a single incoming edge and no inserted code.

But jump optimization would normally delete unused labels before cse1 runs, so a plain goto wouldn't survive. A loop header label (like from a for/while) could work instead, since loop notes keep structural labels alive, though the target's first block doesn't look like a loop itself. Maybe pd's uses are confined entirely to that head block, or there's some other construct where the lui/addiu for a1 is scheduled as the very first instruction in the prologue.

I wonder if making the base address non-constant could dodge cse, but cse folds constant arithmetic anyway, and there's no function call to produce a non-constant pointer. Perhaps pd is actually indexing into something else, or this pattern recurs in a sibling function that I could check against for clues.

I should check the cookbook entries C0505, C0119, C0087, C0287, and C0278 since those likely contain the expert's suggestions relevant to this.

Every one of the 38 head classes got at least one crack attempt, largest first, with up to 4 crack coders running at once (18 coder runs in total). 18 classes now compile byte-identical from plain C with no pins and no asm. Each crack was copied to every overlay that shares the function, 442 copies in all, which removed 586 rows from the lever residue ledger. The ledger, config/lever_residue.tsv, has been rewritten and now holds 1457 data rows.

The work went in four batches, each with a full gate of 218/218:
- c8 removed 400 rows.
- c13 removed 25.
- c16 removed 148.
- c18 removed 13.

On the current HEAD I ran the whole verify line. `lever_census --check --strict --residue` is OK with 0 unused residue rows, and the full R22 gate gives 218 passed, 0 failed of 218 (`.run/logs/t9_final_gate.log`).

The other 20 classes keep their rows. The summary records each one's attempts and best near-miss. The biggest are func_80140958 (258 rows, best was a single-instruction mismatch), func_8013CF68 (134 rows, blocked by how the compiler tracks a known base address) and 413fa955ff17 (265 rows, the target has dead arithmetic).

Agent time was about 21 coder-hours, estimated from the log timestamps, plus three expert attempts.

**Decision for you:** func_80177B5C (133 rows) does match, but only by adding `w = (w >> 16 << 16) | x1;`. That line changes nothing in the program; it is there only to steer the compiler, which is what a launder does, just written in C. Counting it as lever-free would redefine the term, so I did not bank it. If you accept it, one more propagation coder in the same style as c8 removes those 133 rows. I recommend keeping the rows.

## 084659-548r-T10-interim
---
ts: 2026-10-17T14:46:59.464Z
session: a4742bc1-12ac-4649-ab77-694e7315677f
agentId: a13c1de15ca946b2d
role: pa-expert
task: T10
---

blocked

The pilot ran as planned. The projection shows that literal `lever_census --check --strict` 0 cannot be reached by the singleton wave at the developer's cap of 4 concurrent coders, so the milestone needs a ruling.

**What was done.** I drew a stratified sample of 20 singleton functions from the residue TSV, covering every lever kind (pin, launder, barrier, keepalive, instruction, mixed) and body sizes from 13 to 262 lines. The draw is fixed by its seed and saved in `/home/musashi/bfm-decomp/.run/P40/T10/sample.tsv`. Four coders worked on them in parallel, in scratch only, with 40 minutes per function. A fifth coder, working alone, banked the wins.
- **Cracked:** 12 of 20 (60%; 95% interval 39 to 78%). All 12 are banked in commit e984a520d5.
- **Gates:** `lever_census --check --strict --residue` is OK with residue-unused 0 (pins 1143 to 1122, asm 1198 to 1186, residue 2342 to 2309, 20 rows removed). I re-ran R22 on HEAD myself: 218 passed, 0 failed of 218. The cast gate has uncovered 0.

**By kind:**

| Kind | Cracked |
|---|---|
| pin | 4 of 5 |
| keepalive | 2 of 2 |
| barrier | 2 of 3 |
| launder | 2 of 4 |
| mixed | 2 of 4 |
| instruction | 0 of 2 |

Body size showed no trend.

**Cost per function attempted:** about 40k tokens and 7.8 coder-minutes. Banking cost about 7.5k tokens per win. Across all 20 functions there were 847 tries, a median of 20 per win; one function also took 1,440 permutation compiles. Walls usually showed within 3 to 15 minutes.

**What worked:**
- reusing a variable, or reassigning a block-local, to steer cse and local-alloc (6 wins);
- a store in each arm, or one condition in place of goto arms, to drop barriers (4);
- narrower or sibling spellings (s16, register unions, a K&R definition) (4);
- `do { } while (0)` marked `// !FAKE: do-while`, which lever_census counts as an ordinary-C fake, not a lever (2);
- duplicating a statement into both arms, writing a power-of-two remainder out in place, and an in-place tag update (1 each).

**What blocked the other 8:** cse folding constant addresses (3), sched1 hoisting or its birthing boost (2), a callee-saved order that needs a `u32 *` return across 136 TUs (1), results the target computes but never uses, which flow deletes (1; it belongs to a family of 11 functions with the same 7 levers), and a `$sp` stack switch (1).

**Projection for the remaining 430 functions (572 rows):**
- about 258 cracks (range 168 to 335);
- about 172 survivors, roughly 230 rows;
- about 19M tokens;
- about 14 hours of wall time at the 4-coder cap.

Even a complete wave leaves strict red, and the head-class, missing-parameter, asm-macro and GTE residue keeps it red anyway.

**Still open, not from this task:** `type_census --check-casts --residue` still exits 1, on 3 K&R-empty lying declarations and 1 unbacked CAST_NONSTRUCT. They come from T8.c4; T10's diff touches none of them. The banker also had to re-sweep 629 uncovered cast sites left by the T8 and T9 edits to get uncovered to 0.

**Decision needed:**
- (a) Run T11 at the cap (about 14 hours and 19M tokens), and amend the milestone so each surviving row carries its pass, instrument and the named compiler wall, in place of literal strict 0.
- (b) The same amendment, but stop the wave after about 4 hours on the best-yielding kinds (pin, keepalive, barrier).

I recommend (a). For T11, I'd use 20 to 30 minute budgets, give each coder its own scratch prefix, and park the instruction rows and the func_80188C04 family.

Files are in /home/musashi/bfm-decomp/phase-ends/current:
- tasks/T10.md (summary)
- logs/T10.md (full log; coder logs are T10.c1.md to T10.c5.md beside it)

The research report is R3.40-010. The task commit is e50a156b77.

## 084729-014r-claude-answer
---
ts: 2026-10-17T14:47:29.298Z
session: a4742bc1-12ac-4649-ab77-694e7315677f
agentId: ac33bcc12f95b099e
model: claude-opus-5-5[1m]
effort: medium
turnId: b704fb85-213b-42d5-bada-88d73dab8ae4
usage: {input: 8, cacheRead: 204751, cacheWrite: 3979, output: 1525}
---

T10 needs your decision. Its pilot shows that the lever goal, a true zero on the strict lever check, can't be reached this phase. Changing that goal changes the milestone, so it's your call.

**What the pilot did.** A "lever" is a pin or a bit of inline asm we use to force the compiler to produce matching bytes. T10 picked 20 functions that each have their own one-off levers, across every lever kind and body size. It gave 4 coders up to 40 minutes per function to rewrite each one in plain C with the bytes still identical. They cracked 12 of the 20, about 60%. All 12 are committed, and every check is green, including 218/218 byte-identical. Each attempt cost about 40k tokens and 8 minutes of coder time. The 8 that failed all hit a specific compiler behaviour, for example the compiler folding constant addresses or reordering instructions, or a stack switch that plain C can't express.

**The projection for the remaining 430 functions:**

| | Estimate |
|---|---|
| Cracked | about 258 (range 168 to 335) |
| Left over | about 172 functions, roughly 230 rows |
| Cost | about 19M tokens, about 14 hours at your 4-coder cap |

Even a complete wave leaves some levers. Other leftovers also keep the strict check from reaching zero: the missing-parameter pins T7 kept as scratch registers, the asm macros and the GTE instructions. GTE is the PlayStation's geometry coprocessor, and its instructions have no C spelling. So no realistic cap reaches a true zero. That also means the generation's own goal of zero levers would need the same change.

**My recommendation is (a).** Run the full wave at your cap, and change the milestone. Every lever that survives keeps its row, which names the pass, the instrument and the specific compiler wall that stopped it. That replaces "strict check 0". The wave would use shorter budgets of 20 to 30 minutes per function. It would skip the two lever families that look hopeless: the instruction rows, and the group of 11 functions sharing the same 7 levers.

The wave isn't cheap, but about 60% of the attempts turn a lever into plain C. Option (b) costs about a third as much and still gets most of the easy wins. Raising the cap would finish sooner, but it still wouldn't reach zero.

**One loose end, whichever you pick.** The cast check (`type_census --check-casts --residue`) is failing again on 4 sites that came from T8's edits. ## 100713-025r-claude-answer
---
ts: 2026-10-17T16:07:13.556Z
session: a4742bc1-12ac-4649-ab77-694e7315677f
agentId: ac33bcc12f95b099e
model: claude-opus-5-5[1m]
effort: medium
turnId: ee7fbc65-97b8-4a8a-8e3c-c7327a9054ea
usage: {input: 16, cacheRead: 619084, cacheWrite: 7083, output: 2580}
---

None of the cracking is yours to do. "Hand cracking" here means Claude coders rewriting each function by hand in plain C, and that is all agent work.

**How much is left.** T11's wave covers 396 one-off functions: 394 still unattempted plus 2 copies of func_80136824. The expert has split them into 50 batches of about 8, so 4 coders work in parallel in scratch and a fifth commits the wins. The four first batches started a few minutes ago. If T10's rate of about 60% holds, about 240 of the 396 get cracked, and about 160 survive with their compiler wall recorded. After that, T20 only tags the roughly 864 rows that no wave touches. That is classification work, not cracking.

**The OpenRouter harness.** We have one: `tools/api_agent.py` gives an OpenRouter model the same tools a Claude coder gets, plus a compile-and-compare loop. We retired that lane in Phase 31 by choice, not because it was broken. The one measured comparison, on 2026-07-01, found two things:
- **Good drafts:** GLM 5.2 matched 10 of 18 hard functions at the function level, for about $1 in total.
- **Few usable:** only 3 of those 18 passed the full binary check. The function-level comparison it uses ignores which symbol a call or address points to, and the remaining failures were compiler walls that no drafting model can get past.

**Should you try external agents now?** Not inside the running wave. T11 already has 4 coders and one committer working through fixed batches against a shared build. Adding an outside lane mid-wave risks two writers on the same files, and it would muddy T11's numbers.

There's another catch. The harness was built for matching functions from assembly, not for removing levers, so it would need a small adapter. Its prompt and its oracle would have to score "lever-free and still byte-identical".

**The best time is right after T11.** Run a scratch-only bake-off on about 20 of the functions T11 couldn't crack. That's a fair, cheap test: a few dollars on the OpenRouter side, nothing committed, and the full binary check decides. If an outside model cracks what Opus couldn't, that's worth knowing before 3.41. If not, we've confirmed the walls are real.

I'm ready to add that as a small task after T11 if you agree. It needs your OpenRouter key in the environment, which only you can provide. Pick an option in the picker. The queue is moving.

## 104847-035r-claude-answer
---
ts: 2026-10-17T16:48:47.680Z
session: a4742bc1-12ac-4649-ab77-694e7315677f
agentId: ac33bcc12f95b099e
model: claude-opus-5-5[1m]

turnId: 22591948-c82d-4a2b-b612-af26462bd9d3

---

Mostly yes. Building structs as we went would have saved the most, and with no real downside. A strict "pure C only" rule from day one would have cost us, unless it came with an escape hatch. Naming as we went is worth doing only when there's evidence behind the name.

**1. Structs as you go: yes, clearly.** This is the biggest avoidable cost we have. The cleanup has dealt with about 292,000 cast sites that were left as residue, and nearly 12,700 placeholder struct types. Most of those placeholders are the same handful of real structs, found again in 218 separate binaries. If every agent had been told to look up the existing struct and extend it before writing a cast, most of that would never have existed. Doing it during matching is cheap, because the agent already understands the memory it's reading at that moment. Doing it now, Phases 3.38 to 3.40 have spent days of restruct cycles on it.

The one real cost: early on, a canonical struct is wrong in places, and fixing it ripples through every file that uses it. That's still far cheaper than merging the duplicates afterwards.

**2. No levers, pure C: right as the default, wrong as an absolute rule.** The evidence cuts both ways.
- **For it:** T7 found that most pins were really wrong declarations, such as a missing parameter. T10 showed that about 60% of the remaining levers fall to plain C when an agent is given 20 to 40 minutes.
- **Against it:** the other 40% hit real compiler walls. Examples are constant-address folding, instructions the compiler moves ahead of where they're written, a stack switch, and GTE (the PlayStation's geometry coprocessor) instructions with no C spelling. A strict ban would have left those functions unmatched, or had agents burning hours on them, and 100% matching would have come much later.

The rule I'd use from the start: a lever is allowed only when the agent records, at the moment it matches the function, which wall forced it and what it tried first. That's exactly the "survivors name their wall" rule you just chose for 3.40, applied at matching time instead of afterwards. It keeps the agents honest without blocking progress.

**3. Understanding the function and naming as you go: partly.** Human decomp projects do this, and it's why their code reads well. For us, there's a catch. A name guessed with no evidence is worse than a placeholder, because people trust it. That's why the 3.43 plan says "unnamed beats wrong".

So I'd ask agents to name only what they can back up: parameters and locals whose role is obvious from the code, and struct fields confirmed by more than one function. Everything else keeps its placeholder until real evidence turns up, such as strings, the debug menu, or watching the game's memory live.

**The trade-off overall.** A "human way" setup would have made the road to 100% slower, maybe by a third, judging by how long the cleanup phases have taken. In exchange, most of Phases 3.35 to 3.40 wouldn't have been needed. For a project starting today, I'd take that trade.

**Where this goes.** T14 already adds the "do sooner" lessons to the from-the-start guide, `docs/how-to-ai-decomp/03-bootstrap-order.md`: canonical types during matching, declarations before pins, and a debt chart from day one. Two points from your question aren't in it yet: levers only with their wall recorded, and names only with evidence. I'd add both to T14. It's a small docs change and doesn't touch the milestone. Pick an option in the picker.

None of those quite: each coder gets a batch of about 8 functions and keeps its context for the whole batch. The 30-minute limit applies per function, not per coder. When one function hits the limit, the coder records its wall and moves on to the next, and it's retired only when its batch is done.

**How T11 groups the work.** Not by kind. The expert sorted the 396 functions by size and dealt them out in turn across 50 batches, so a pin, a launder and a barrier can all land in the same batch.

**Exemplar and propagate.** We did do that, in T9. It cracked one example from each of the 38 lever classes that repeat across many functions, and its fix covered every copy. What's left for T11 are the singletons: functions whose lever text appears nowhere else. So there is no exact solution to copy. That's why T11 cracks them one at a time.

**Your warm-context idea is still right.** Singletons of the same kind tend to fall to the same few moves. In T10, about half the wins came from two tricks:
- reusing a variable or a block-local to steer the compiler's choices (6 wins);
- putting a store in each branch of an if, instead of using a barrier (4 wins).

A coder that has just cracked three barriers with the second trick will crack a fourth barrier faster than a new coder would. Grouping functions from the same source file helps as well, because the coder has already read that file's types and headers.

The cost is modest. A coder's context grows with each function, but the re-read part is cached and costs about a tenth of fresh input. So 8 to 12 related functions per coder is a good range. Much longer than that, and old notes start to crowd out the current function.

**My recommendation.** Have T11's expert re-deal the 43 batches nobody has started yet. Each batch would hold one lever kind, and functions from the same file where possible. Every coder would also add the moves that worked to one shared notes file, which the next coder reads first. That spreads what's learned across coders, not just within one. The 7 batches already running carry on unchanged, so nothing is lost.

It doesn't change the cap or the milestone, only how T11 deals its work. Pick an option in the picker.

## 110012-045r-claude-answer
---
ts: 2026-10-17T17:00:12.481Z
session: a4742bc1-12ac-4649-ab77-694e7315677f
agentId: ac33bcc12f95b099e

---

I agree, and your naming idea is better than the rule I gave you. "Don't name until sure" does leave a late-stage decomp full of `func_8001234` and `unk10`. A marked best guess carries real information and doesn't claim more than it knows.

**It's written down.** I've added all three rules to T14. They go into the wiki pages "Start a new decomp project" and "How to AI decomp", and into the bootstrap guide, as rules for any new project using our workflow:
1. **Structs as you go.** Before writing any raw cast, an agent looks up the existing struct and extends it.
2. **Pure C by default.** A lever is allowed only with its compiler wall, and what was tried first, recorded when the function is matched.
3. **Best-guess names from the start**, under a guess prefix, promoted to a plain name once there's evidence.

Each rule cites what skipping it cost us in Phases 3.35 to 3.40. The change goes in at the next task boundary. I've also noted your naming preference on the card (`HOW_WE_WORK.md`), so later planners see it.

**What I'd add to make the naming rule work:**
- **A distinct prefix.** Decomp convention already uses `unk` for "nothing known": `unk10` fields, `Unkstruct_80126B58`, and so on. If `unk_MutateHp` meant "best guess", the two meanings would blur. A separate marker like `guess_MutateHp`, or `maybe_`, keeps three clear tiers: placeholder, guess and confirmed. Whichever prefix you pick, the important thing is that it means one thing only.
- **A names ledger.** Each guessed name gets one row saying why: "writes the player HP field, called from the damage handler". Promoting a guess to a plain name then means adding evidence to its row. Without the row, a guess that has survived long enough starts to look like a fact. The ledger also guards against the one real risk, a wrong guess steering later agents. Agents are told that guessed names are guesses, and the ledger shows how weak each one is.
- **Cheap renames.** 3.42 already builds the tool that proves every reference to a renamed symbol was rewritten, so changing a guess later costs very little.

**On helping the cracking: yes, but indirectly.** The compiler ignores names, so a name never changes the bytes. What helps is the agent's understanding. An agent that knows it's touching `guess_PlayerState.hp` rather than `*(s16*)(a0+0x4C)` picks the right types and widths sooner, and those do change the code the compiler generates. That's the same reason structs as you go helps.

**For this project.** 3.43 is still planned as "unnamed beats wrong": only names with evidence, and everything else stays a placeholder. Your idea would change that phase's milestone, so it's your call. I recommend adopting it with a distinct prefix and the ledger. 3.43 would then name far more than its top 500, and every guess would still be marked as one. Pick an option in the picker.

Best-guess naming with the `guess_` prefix is now the project's rule.

- **The standard.** I've amended rule 1 in `docs/gen3-standards.md`. A name without evidence is now allowed as a best guess under `guess_`, such as `guess_MutateHp`, `guess_PlayerState` or a field `guess_hp`. Each guess gets a names-ledger row giving its basis and confidence. `guess_` always means "our best guess so far". `unk`, `Unkstruct_` and `func_` keep meaning "nothing known". A guess drops the prefix only once there's recorded evidence, and it can be renamed freely before then.
- **The generation plan.** 3.42's rename tools and names ledger now carry the guess tier. 3.43 is now "Evidence and best-guess names". Confirmed names need a citation row and guesses need a basis row, and the 3.42 scanner checks both. `no-evidence` is now only for names where not even a guess has a basis.
- **The kit docs.** Two docs for new projects still say "unnamed beats wrong": `decomp-architect/decomp-architect.md` and `decomp-architect/intake.decomp.md`. T14 updates them along with the wiki pages.

The rule changes are committed as fe8028d960 and the link check is clean. Both commits I made earlier today also took in the journal and T11's coder logs, which is harmless.

---
ts: 2026-10-17T17:09:58.783Z
session: a4742bc1-12ac-4649-ab77-694e7315677f
agentId: ac33bcc12f95b099e
model: claude-opus-5-5[1m]

---

The 3.43 milestone change is already in. I made it in my last message, committed as fe8028d960.

**Prefixes: I'd keep one in the name and put the rest in the ledger.** You're right that confidence has levels, and knowing how we worked something out matters. But the name is the wrong place for it, for three reasons:
- **Every step becomes a rename.** If `guess_` becomes `likely_`, every file that uses the name changes. With hundreds of names moving up over time, the code history fills with renames that change no meaning.
- **"Maybe" is a different question.** It means "the evidence conflicts", not "less sure than likely". Squeezing both into one prefix ladder blurs them.
- **Readers can't tell the levels apart.** Someone reading `maybe_` next to `likely_` can't say how much more sure one is than the other. A ledger row can.

So the code shows only the one thing a reader must know: `guess_` (not confirmed) or a plain name (confirmed). The ledger row holds the detail:
- **Confidence:** low, medium or high.
- **Evidence kind:** inferred from the code, string, cross-reference chain, debug menu, live-RAM observation, or a community label.
- **A conflict flag** for your "maybe" case, naming the contradicting evidence.
- **Name history**, so we know what it was called before.

A tool can list "all guesses with conflicts" or "all high-confidence guesses ready to confirm" whenever we want. If you'd still rather have the levels in the name, it works. It just costs more renames. I put it in the first picker.

**Comments as you go, from day 1: yes, strongly.** It's the same lesson as structs and names. Understanding is cheapest to record at the moment an agent has it. An agent that has just matched a function knows what it does, why an odd spelling is there and what it tried. Two lines of comment then cost almost nothing, while recovering that later costs a full agent run. Comments don't change the compiled bytes, so the 218/218 check is unaffected.

You're right that older comments may be wrong. That's fine: comments get edited freely, and git keeps the old versions. I'd only ask that a guess in a comment says it's a guess, the same way names do.

**Going back now: no whole-codebase comment pass, and no transcript-mining phase.** My reasons:
- **A comment pass would mostly produce noise.** An agent that reads code only to comment it, without the understanding that comes from working on it, writes "loops over the array" style comments. Many would be confidently wrong.
- **The transcripts are the wrong source.** Most of the reasoning in them is about how to match the compiler: register pins, compiler pass quirks, permuter runs. Hardly any is about what the game code means. The parts that matter are already distilled: the cookbook, the restruct ledger, each lever's recorded wall, and the `!FAKE` notes. Reading them would also cost a great many tokens, on a scale like this whole phase, for a little value.

**What I'd do for this project instead:**
1. **Write comments as part of naming in 3.43.** Whoever names a function or field also writes a short header comment: what it does, how sure we are, the basis. That's when the understanding actually exists.
2. **A cheap ledger-to-comment pass.** A small tool copies what the ledgers already know into the code: each surviving lever gets a one-line comment naming its compiler wall, and each guessed name points to its ledger row. That's mechanical, nearly free, and puts the reasoning where a reader will see it.
3. **Day 1 for new projects:** add "comment as you go" to T14's guide, next to structs as you go, pure C and guess names.

Both choices are in the pickers.

**How hard the remaining T11 work is.** About average, and it's going better than the pilot. So far 73 of the 396 functions have results:

| Result | Count |
|---|---|
| Cracked | 37 (51%) |
| Partly cracked (one lever kind left) | 10 |
| Compiler wall | 26 |

That's in line with T10's 60% once you count the partial cracks, and cheaper. Opus is using about 20k tokens and 4 to 5 minutes per function, half of T10's 40k. The cookbook moves and the shared notes file seem to be helping.

The remaining 323 are a similar mix, because the first batches were dealt across all sizes. By lever kind:
- **Single-kind, the easier end:** about 107 pins, 96 launders, 63 barriers and 26 keepalives.
- **Two or more kinds at once:** about 100, which crack less often.

At the current pace T11 should finish in about 6 to 8 hours, quicker than the 14 projected.

**Haiku for all of it: not yet, and probably not for everything.** Its sample is only 5 functions so far: 2 cracked, 1 partly cracked, 2 walls. That's too few to bet the wave on.

There's also a hidden cost. When Haiku hits a wall, we can't tell whether it's a real compiler wall or just Haiku giving up. Every Haiku wall would need an Opus retry to be sure, so we'd pay twice on those.

A tiered approach gets most of the savings without that risk, and the project used it in earlier matching waves:
- Haiku takes the small single-kind functions first.
- Opus retries only the functions Haiku couldn't crack.
- Opus takes the large and multi-kind functions directly.

Every Haiku crack still goes through the same committer and the 218/218 check, so wrong code can't get in. The only risk is wasted tries.

**My recommendation.** Wait until Haiku's 10 functions are finished, which should be within the hour. If it cracks at least about 40%, switch the unstarted single-kind batches to Haiku first, with Opus retrying its walls. If it cracks less than that, keep Opus. Pick an option in the picker.

If you can, aim for 15 GB or more before T11 resumes. `.run/` is the big one, at about 40 GB, and much of it is old phase scratch that can be regenerated.

---

PA3 3.29 is out. It's a tidy-up release with four changes:

- **Quieter keep-warm pings.** A keep-warm ping is the small message the mod sends a waiting agent so its cache doesn't expire. Those pings no longer repeat the agent's "Starting:" line, either in the main session, in what I read with your next message, or as a journal entry every few minutes.
- **My row in the band.** My row now appears as soon as you send a message, reading "received". Once I start, it shows what I'm doing, and it goes away when I answer.
- **One temp folder per phase.** Each phase keeps its scratch work in `.run/<phase>/`, including the logs from `tools/run.sh`. That folder is temporary only, and anything the project needs lives in its proper place in the repo. Every phase plan now ends with a cleanup task that checks the folder, and the phase close deletes it.
- **A one-time cleanup offer.** On the first start on 3.29, I'll offer to clean up the older work in `.run/`. I check it against the plans, the phase records and the code that uses it, then show you what I'd keep, rebuild or delete. Nothing is deleted until you say yes. This fits your housekeeping: `.run/` is about 40 GB of the disk.

This update also changed a setting that only takes effect after a restart, and it changed the mod's own files. So the new mod and agents load only when Claude Code starts again. You'll pick up where you left off, and no task starts until then. T11 stays held as well.

Project Architect 3.29, a feature update (The tidy-up), is installed.

**To load it: /exit, then claude --continue (not /clear)**
